ParlaMint-AT_2019-01-30-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00060_d8e6472	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen at the gallery! The current European hour is entitled "After the Brexit Debakel: Now is the chance to start Europe again!” Well, I can do much to get the idea of understanding Brexit as an opportunity, because this is undoubtedly a positive and optimistic approach to a situation which is not very positive anyway. We in the Federal Government have been working on the future of the Union since the beginning of our accession and have been preparing our Presidency for this. The effects of the Brexit are enormous, all of them have now come to mind, especially the British. It is now also a question of how the relationship between Europe and the UK will continue if the British leave the Union and how it manages to keep the damage to the remaining 27 as low as possible. Oh, yes, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no, no. I grew up at a time when there was ever more European integration in which one country submitted an application for accession after another. But suddenly this linear development of an increasingly integrated Europe is due to the vote on 24 March. It was disrupted and destroyed on 1 June 2016. The British have decided to go. At that time, many commentators wrote that this will be the beginning of the end of the common Union, because the British are now speaking with one voice and the raisins will pick themselves out of the cake and the other 27 of the Union will disunite and not find a common line. – Good thing it didn’t come that way! One of the key challenges during the Austrian Presidency was to maintain this unity of the EU-27. We had more than five Brexit formats in the Council, we kept in close contact with Michel Barnier every week to discuss what we can do to ensure that unity is maintained, and I am glad to say that during our presidency this has also been well done. What can happen has been seen at the first Council under the Romanian Presidency, which has already made its first voices loud to those who have adopted a little of this common course. It is therefore important to continue to work on maintaining the unity of the 27. What is the status quo? . Yesterday we were allowed to pursue all day long how the votes in the British Parliament are going to take place. There is still no final clarity as to what Britain wants. It's Now a little clearer what the British do not want: They don't want a hard Brexit, but they don't want this deal that's been negotiated either. So we know what they don't want, we don't know much more about what they really want, so the ball is still with the UK. I am only warning against giving the Union the impression that we are now trying to reinvigorate this Treaty. We will certainly not: It has been long, well-negotiated, this is a compromise from both sides, and the Brexit is anyway a loose-free situation, which can only be a little mitigated with this treaty. We in Austria are also preparing intensively for a possible hard Brexit. Today, in the Council of Ministers, we have adopted a Brexit-Preparedness Act – a collective law in which about 15 laws from eight different resorts have been brought in to regulate and resolve things that can happen if there is a hard Brexit. This concerns, for example, the question of what is happening to British citizens from the beginning of April: Can they still stay here and work or not? . We say yes, they should continue to be allowed to do so, analogous to a scheme that also applies to 25 000 Austrians in Great Britain. I think you should consider the Brexit as an opportunity to understand it very cleverly, but you should also consider why the Brexit came about. One essential aspect, for example, was the fear of migration. That was one of the main reasons why the British have said that they want their sovereignty, their control back. If we want to learn something from Brexit, then we have to deal directly with migration problems in Europe, because otherwise we will not learn anything from Brexit. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and FPÖ.]] That is why, during the Presidency of the Council, we have done everything we can to move the challenge of migration a little further in the right direction – in order to take into account what Brexit has shown us, namely that many people are afraid of irregular migration. It also needs many more building blocks, such as the pre-accession of the Western Balkans to the European Union. That's a very central issue. Why? – This region is right in front of the gates of Europe and is historical, but also economically relevant for Austria, and we can decide whether we are either exporting stability to this region by showing a clear prospect of accession, or whether we are importing instability when we close the door. That is not what we want, and that is why, during the Presidency of the Council, we have worked intensively to ensure that the approach takes place, and we will continue to do so. [[Congratulations to the ÖVP and to Mr Belakovich, Stefan and Mr Zanger.]] My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen, there is a need for a Europe that solves the problems in the large, and no more centralism. [[Congratulations to the ÖVP and to Mr Belakovich, Stefan and Mr Zanger.]] We need realism, a way of dealing with it, which is a hard-hit policy, where we look exactly where people have needs, concerns and fears, and solve these problems. Europe is no longer a dream – luckily! – Europe is reality. Those who continue to dream here will lose this reality again. Thank you.

On the one hand, with Donald Trump, there is a president who is unpredictable and Europe is driving through tweets all the time, and on the other hand, with Russia and China, there are two equally unpredictable actors, both of whom are constantly uplifting. As a Europe, of course, it must be in our sense to invest in a common defence in order to ensure long-term security. Of course, the basis for this common defence must always be solidarity within Europe, peacekeeping, because Europe has been a great peace project from the beginning and is still a very important issue, and, of course, respect for human rights is always a matter. This must be the pillars that we have taken on a European defence and security policy. If we are then claimed to be neutral and neutrality secures us, then unfortunately I have to say that is not so anymore. It's not like cyberattacks, when we put on the border a sign with the inscription neutrality, then suddenly stop, according to the motto: I would rather not go over this border now, because this is a neutral country! – Neutrality alone is not a guarantee of security, and therefore we have to take further steps – we are even in the duty – and we have to continue thinking and develop a long-term perspective regarding a common European defence and security policy – of course, Mr. Schieder – of a European army, because only in this way will we be able to guarantee long-term security. [[Abg. Arbitrators: But not an army either!]] [[Abg. Arbitrator: Is not a guarantee either!]] The fact is, there are many conflicts in Europe, in the neighbouring countries, even if it does not directly affect our borders, and it would be a lie to say that they do not concern us, that is not at our border, we are unaffected by all that happens. Of course, the impact on us, and we all know that we are still regularly discussing the consequences of the refugee crisis. Of course, these conflicts have an influence on us and, accordingly, we must act together here, we must see the whole thing and keep it together more closely as Europe. If a conflict does not directly affect our borders, it does not mean that it cannot endanger our security, our prosperity and our peace, so we must take steps there. However, a common security and defence policy also has many other advantages, such as a financial one. The EU-28 – 28 more – spends a total of EUR 227 billion annually on defence policy. The European Commission says that we can save a lot of money. According to calculations, it is between 25 and 100 billion euros that can be saved there; for example, joint airspace monitoring has always been a topic. The intelligence services are a very important area in which we still have problems, because they all pay far too much attention to their nation and work far too little together, cooperate too little. There are regular terrorist attacks, and we know that in the last 23 terrorist attacks in Europe, at least one suspect in any European country has already been known and the local Constitutional Protectors have been known. Because we don't work well enough, we haven't been able to prevent these things. Especially when security is important to us, we need to see the European perspective and work on this European perspective. All in all, I think, it's important that we go forward courageously now. We are – unfortunately – apparently the only ones who courageously go forward and say: We want to take the next steps, we want this Europe to be a place of peace in the long term, and to invest accordingly in a common security and defence policy! Only a strong Europe can remain operational, and that is particularly important to us.

In concrete terms, we are removing a regulation that has proved unnecessary: We abandon the issue of concession certificates in the national field. To date, it has been necessary to issue this document as compulsory for national road transport, but this concession certificate does not contain any essential points which are not already mentioned in the notice, and there is no obligation to carry them. The issue of this certificate is time-consuming and counter-productive for the authorities, so it is not possible to do so. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and FPÖ.]] With the second point, we are also contributing to simplification, which is about the stop-off procedure, which is to be facilitated. Austria is becoming more and more a country for public transporters: More than four million people use the bus next to the train. Of course, bus stops are particularly important here, because their arrangement and design influence the attractiveness of public passenger transport. The first is to meet the needs of passengers, secondly to meet the requirements of road safety and, thirdly, to fit the road and city map with adequate space. Also a key point: They must not limit individual traffic too much. Of course, the authorisation and authorisation procedure is of great importance, because safety is also a top priority. The fixing of stops is therefore the responsibility of the national heads of state or of national heads of state. Up to now, a new authorisation procedure had to be carried out after each change of the operator of a power line. With the change that our Minister has now begun, the head of state or the head of state can now refrain from an investigation procedure if the stop had already been approved for the power line operation of a passenger transport undertaking. With this change, we save our communities time and a lot of money, and we simplify their work to the authorities. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and FPÖ.]] Simplification is the motto of this change that we are meeting here. I would like to thank the Committee for its unanimity and also for its approval in plenary. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2019-01-30-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00060_d8e21668	Madam President. (Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) Ladies and gentlemen in the gallery and in front of the screens! There is a system in Austria that distinguishes us from other countries and brings great benefits to our country. This is the system of voluntary service organizations, whether it be the fire brigade, whether it be the rescue of the mountain. This and many others have given our country the opportunity to promote security, stability and good development for our citizens. This voluntaryity is not a matter of course. We are living at a time when, on the one hand, the willingness to volunteer for others is declining, on the other hand, it is becoming more and more difficult to find volunteers because of the pressure in work and other things. We often experience in fire brigades that the members are getting less. We have recently heard that in the area of the Red Cross, the number of civilians is too low and the volunteers no longer take up their service in the frequency, as was the case before. [[Abg. Hanger: That's something quite different, Mr. Cot!]] [[Abg. Black: You're confusing something!]] [[Abg. Rosary: You can fix it again!]] [[Abg. Hanger: Civil servants are not volunteer!]] – This is not a confusion, Mr colleague, these are things that complement each other and do not make the overall situation easier for the intervention organisations. I think we agree. This applies not only to city intervention organisations, but also to the country, it will be more difficult to find volunteers working in this area. These volunteers are willing to express themselves by day and by night when there are traffic accidents and when other problems arise. It is these men and women who voluntarily, often using their own lives, put themselves at risk, clarifying the situation and ensuring safety, ladies and gentlemen. We have recently experienced, especially in Central Austria, in the Alps, in the Upper Styria and in other areas, that it would have been impossible without these volunteers getting hold of the situation in such a way that no major problems occurred. The massive snowfall was only by many, many volunteers to bring under control. In the end, the cut-off villages there could only be reached by the use of many, many volunteers. It was necessary to scoop on roofs for days so that houses did not collapse under these snow masses. I think we also agree that these volunteers often go to the limits of their personal capacity to help other people. In an interview the Federal Fire Department recently also said that the fire brigades have arrived at the limits of their performance in the current operations. Ladies and gentlemen, our society depends on these volunteers. They sacrifice their free time, they also often sacrifice their holidays, if it is not possible to reach a different agreement with the service provider to work there. [[Abg. Black: Eminence!]] I believe that it is necessary for us as the political responsible, as legislators, to consider how it is possible to help these volunteers better, as it is possible to ensure legal certainty for them, and how it is possible if these efforts take days to create legal certainty for all parties involved. It's a difficult situation for both sides, you have to say that quite openly. It is a difficult situation for those involved and also a difficult situation for the service providers who, of course, depend on these people or the work performance at that time. Ladies and gentlemen, there is no way by: Our volunteers, who sacrifice for our country, need a legal, a labour security. This is the situation that I think is relatively quick to clarify from us as a legislator for these volunteers. The SPÖ proposes a legal change which will allow volunteers to obtain a right of exemption from work for up to five working days a year and which will also ensure the further payment of the salary at that time. This is intended to apply to all voluntary and voluntary members of disaster relief services, rescue services, voluntary fire brigades and all other organisations dependent on these volunteers. But it also needs – I think we will agree – a guarantee for the service providers that the loss of their earnings from this time when workers can take advantage of this holiday will also be paid off. There is our proposal for the reimbursement from the disaster fund. Ladies and gentlemen, it is time these tens of thousands, no, hundreds of thousands of volunteers, who show commitment to our country, were insured under labour law when they did. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] It is time for entrepreneurs who do not have easy access to this situation to be financially compensated for it. I think it's really time we did something here. Not least the snow situation in Austria has shown: We depend on these people. Our country is dependent on these people, and that is why we also have to ensure that they can perform their service, their performance securely. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2019-01-30-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00060_d8e24148	Today a year ago it became public that in Upper Austria the so-called I classes are not continued. Until then, one has not known the difference, not knowing that in Upper Austria the practice of the I-Classes is understood differently from in all other federal states. As a Parliament, I have not been very concerned about the subject in recent years either. Why are I classes so important? – They are important because in special schools children with disabilities and children without impairment learn a better understanding of each other in common. They are a place where children with disabilities consider it to be of course that those who have no impairment are still their friends. They are not separated, they experience normal growth. They give parents who have a child with disabilities and a child without impairment the opportunity to send their children, the two siblings, to the same school. This has a high priority for family life, and this also has very practical advantages. All of this should no longer be available to other children in Upper Austria or to smaller ones who have not yet been schooled. This has been experienced by parents in Upper Austria. We have, and this is a very important point, since the affected also belong. I can get a victim in front of the curtain. Barbara Hofer, a affected mother and spokesperson of the initiative that turned to the Committee on Petitions, told us her story. She is a graduate nurse, mother of three children, her middle son is severely affected, and she wants her children to go to school together in Steyr. This is only possible if there are further I-Classes. One has to say that it is not possible to rely on state or federal policy, because when the public was announced that there should no longer be the I-Classes, there was no cry in national politics, there were excuses. At first, there was no cry in federal politics, because it was only when those affected reported. So it was up to those concerned, among other things, to do something to Mrs Hofer, who founded the I-Class I-Class initiative. They have literally walked from Pontius to Pilate, they have contacted NGOs, they have con- They have contacted federal politics, they have contacted the President of the Federal Republic of Germany, they have contacted the Ministry, and then something has been successful, which unfortunately does not succeed in all the initiatives. It has succeeded in drawing attention to a subject both media and politically, so that it has come to a solution. The solution on the part of the Federal Ministry has looked quite specific so that we continue to have I-Classes in Upper Austria until 2021. We know from the reply to this question in the opinions to the Committee on Petitions that all other federal states do not have this problem in the present form. Why don't they? – For a simple reason: because they interpret the compulsory school organisation law differently. That is, in eight federal states, what does not work in the ninth federal state. Now the question is: Are eight wrong or is this a wrong one? What we need now, and this is not yet the task of the Committee on Petitions, is that the initiative, which is also under negotiation directly with the country of Upper Austria, can move the country of Upper Austria to realize that it is the same as all the other eight federal states. If that doesn't work, I can say that you have some allies in the National Council that you can discuss the subject again here. I would like to stress two things, and this is very important in my view: We have seen that the commitment of the initiative has paid off, that if you really organize yourself, if you pursue a subject with emphasis, commitment can also lead to success. Ladies and gentlemen, and above all appreciated citizens' initiative, I would like to highlight one thing, which is very important: We're talking about directly concerned, we're talking about children, we're talking about children and families suffering because a federal country is different from everyone else. In conclusion, two things: First of all, ladies and gentlemen, let us see together that we too, Upper Austria, with much love and if necessary with much emphasis, are heading in the right direction! And my dear citizens' initiative: Please continue until you reach your goal! Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2019-01-30-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00060_d8e24655	Madam President. High house! I will briefly comment on two citizens' initiatives. The first is the Citizens' Initiative on Parental Xenophobia. Parents who deliberately alienate the child are required to be prosecuted against the other parent. I don't have to tell you that the good parent relationship with both parents is a very big concern for us. In order to achieve this objective, there are already several possibilities and measures in Austria. The most important thing to say about this is that after a separation, a code of conduct is provided for by law for parents. On the contrary, the courts have the opportunity to restrict or withdraw the care. It is even possible to compensate for damages, forcibles and evasions, and there is also a criminal provision for the withdrawal of children. The government is also committed to prevention, consultation, mediation and visitation. Further criminal intensification would contribute to an escalation and harm the child's well-being. Therefore, we do not want to meet the demands of this citizens' initiative on this issue, we take note of it. Yes, fathers have rights, and yes, we know the situations – how painful they are and how desperate the parents, the fathers and the mothers, are often. But the child's well-being is in the foreground. The focus is always on the child who needs the relationship with both parents, even if it hurts, even if it is difficult for the parents. The government programme will provide for further measures which we will also implement in the coming years. The second Citizens' Initiative “We Austrians do not want organs from China for which innocent people have been killed” calls on the National Council to condemn illegal organ withdrawals in China and to stop organ transplant tourism. This is an important global concern. The laws in Austria are good and sufficient. We know, however, that the situation in China is not good. In 2016, the European Parliament adopted a report stating that up to 1 million people have been killed by illegal organ withdrawals in China over the past 15 years. Surprisingly, the waiting period for organ transplants in China is very short and much shorter compared to other countries. The origin of thousands of donor organs in China is unclear. We want to send this citizens' initiative to the Human Rights Committee and to look at it so that Austria can set a clear mark against organ trafficking and illegal organ withdrawal. I would like to thank the first Signatories of both citizens' initiatives and all other Signatories for their work, for their commitment. There's a lot of heart behind it. In this way you can carry to a living Democracy! Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2019-01-30-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00060_d8e25320	Madam President. Madam President of the Court of Auditors! Ladies and gentlemen! It is a fact that the presence of nursing homes in nursing homes would not be sufficient, there would be no 24-hour care. This 24-hour care is provided by women, mainly women from the former Eastern block states, who return to Austria for 14 days or four weeks, and then return to their homes. This 24-hour care is supported, and therefore the Court of Auditors has examined the 24-hour care for the period 2013 to 2015 in the federal states of Vienna and Upper Austria. In this audit, the Court of Auditors has found shortcomings in the management, control of the conditions for support and the seriousness of the mediation agencies. Indeed, the most important position is given to the mediation agencies in this triangle – the person cared for, the supervisor and the mediation agency. They're both dependent on them. It is therefore necessary to ensure that these mediation agencies are serious. The Court of Auditors proposes the introduction of a seal of quality for serious mediation agencies. It also proposes that, in order to ensure the quality of care, compulsory home visits be provided by qualified caregivers. In order to control the conditions for funding – and this is not enough – the Court of Auditors recommends that an IT application be introduced and applied. These are important recommendations, and I am convinced that they should be taken into account when the big care reform comes, because without this 24-hour care, a durable, quality and legal care at home will not be possible. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2019-01-30-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00060_d8e25375	Madam President. In my speech to my predecessor Dr. Griss, I would like to join in full. The fact that the 24-hour care is a major construction site in the Austrian care system is not only my criticism, it has also been confirmed to us by the current report we are now considering. One of the most important problems that have been addressed is the lack of training for caregivers. In other words, there are not only domestic or lifestyle assistance, but also care activities for which there would have to be an instruction by a doctor, by a qualified health power, which is then recommended. In spite of all this, however, I think it is urgently necessary, in our opinion, to establish minimum standards in training, for example, a minimum training such as that of the home helper, who is already in existence in Austria. A second major criticism was the lack of knowledge of the language of the caregivers. Often there is only the condition of the mediation agencies that any type of certificate must be presented, a short course, however, but these foreign certificates are not helpful to the maintainer himself or to the person to be cared for, because at the end of the day it is about communication, and if this communication cannot take place in sufficient way, there is also a problem. That is, what we need is minimum standards of communication, minimum language skills, that is, a certain quality criterion. We would certainly have opportunities here in Austria, among other things we have the Austrian language diploma as a quality criterion, which we could define as a minimum condition. A third point – and I think this is actually the most serious criticism that the Court of Auditors has found in the 24-hour service – is that, and I would like to quote, ‘intrepid pricing’ of care costs; the Court of Auditors is literally referring to ‘circumstance contracts’, which are to be committed and stopped to the caretakers. That is to say, a situation is being exploited in which the needy caregivers take this path to care for our older fellow citizens here in Austria and also, as we know from the Court of Auditors' report, to care for them. It is precisely this need that is being exploited, and these people are also subject to a flawed contract, from which you can hardly find out and which act like a collection function – actually an example of sheer indifference. The scandal is – that is why it is the most serious problem for me – that we invest incredible sums of funding in this area of care – 60 percent of the funds come from the federal government, 40 percent of the funds come from the countries – but do not have any control at all. Where do these resources flow? Is a quality standard from a health perspective really guaranteed for our older fellow citizens? This also refers to working conditions. All of this is currently working together with a minister, and that is why I really want to make improvements, and we will be bringing forward proposals and proposals in the context of the reform of care in the next few months in order to achieve them. As has been said, it is about the four main points: the training of staff members, the lack of language skills, the flawed contracts imposed on the caregivers, and the intruding pricing as the fourth major criticism, including the persons to be cared for. That is, every family facing the situation that it needs a 24-hour care, because the health situation does not allow anything else, then, as the Court of Auditors says, it still has to fight to ensure that there is an opaque pricing, the mediation costs on the one hand, and the costs for the caregivers on the other. In other words, one could really come to the idea of introducing a seal of goodness, as proposed by the Court of Auditors, but unfortunately we are currently seeing: While Federal Minister Hartinger-Klein proposes a seal of quality, a quality criterion, as a parliamentary request has been made by our group, the Minister says that those agencies, who themselves are allowed to race through this test for the quality seal and do not fulfil these criteria, must nevertheless have access to the Austrian market. In my opinion, this is not transparent, it is not responsible to our older fellow citizens. We must guarantee minimum standards, because in this - - - yes, I would like to agree with my colleague Jarolim, it is criminal to continue to support and further promote this area by public means, which is so built on the backs of people in need of care, but also caregivers. [[Abg. Jarolim: It's almost criminal!]] It requires mandatory legal quality criteria and quality standards. Otherwise, we will not be able to limit the mafia structures in this area. Please go along here, and I also hope to have a mandatory legal regime in this area! Thank you.

How does the AMS work at all? – The labour market service has been distributed, is not subject to the competence of the federal government, but there is a board of directors who is socially employed, and this Board of Directors decides on the use of the funds made available by the federal government to the AMS. [[Interlocutory calls from Mr Greiner and Mr Jarolim.]] Interesting in this context is always: If the labour market service makes decisions in its own field, then the federal government is criticized, although the federal government does not make the decisions but the social partnership body. So, and now on the clear criticisms of the Court of Auditors: The Court of Auditors has found that the influence of the federal government in the Board of Directors has failed to fulfil the task of the AMS. Due to the high demand, funding paid throughout Germany has increased from EUR 9.14 million to EUR 138.75 million since the introduction of this measure from 2008 to 2015, with the federal government taking over 60 percent of the benefits and taking over 40 percent of the countries. The Court of Auditors therefore reviewed the promotion of 24-hour care from October 2016 to March 2017, focusing on the countries of Upper Austria and Vienna. The aim was to assess the financing of the 24-hour service, promotion and quality assurance. Ladies and gentlemen, the quality assurance system needs to be extended even further in the area of 24-hour care. It is also necessary that, regardless of the type of qualification of the caregiver, compulsory home visits are carried out by qualified nursing staff. We can thus ensure that the caregivers do not take care of or take on medical activities. At this point I would like to thank our Minister for Social Affairs Beate Hartinger-Klein, because the recommendations are already being implemented. I am particularly pleased that a quality seal is being developed, which should allow for a better comparability of the various providers of a 24-hour service. This enables us to find a qualified, reliable and competent 24-hour care force not only for those concerned, but also for their families. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2019-01-30-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00060_d8e26228	Mr President. Dear ladies and gentlemen! High house! First of all, I would like to take a brief look at the entire Court of Auditors reports on the agenda today: We have submitted 22 reports from the Court of Auditors, which are to be debated here, of which seven follow-up reviews and a special audit on capital income tax refunds after dividend distributions – that was a request of the Green Club at the time. I would also like to say that it is our task to examine independently and objectively. On the basis of our audit reports and on the basis of this criticism, the Court of Auditors is concerned with achieving improvements in all the areas we examine. The specific criticisms can be found in detail in the reports we have presented. Let me, however, address a number of key recommendations that the Court of Auditors must make again today. I see it as the responsibility of the Ministries – they are called to do so – to respond immediately to these criticisms and to take the necessary measures. The Court of Auditors, for its part, provides sound foundations, and it is now in the hands of policy-makers to actively address them. There are eight specific fields of action that can be seen in our test reports. The first topic is the quality of public services. This is also the Court's audit focus, and this audit focusses on the benefits of citizens. For example, the report on the AMS on training and job placement in the field of active labour market policy, the report on dentistry – which we are still discussing – with regard to a current supply through a new overall contract, the report on quality assurance in the established area on uniform criteria for the quality of treatment for all patients and also the 24-hour report on care, which was addressed here, inter alia, on extending the quality assurance system and mandatory home visits that we have requested. The seal of goodness has already been addressed. The second point, which is important for the Court of Auditors, is the creation of modern organisational structures. In the AMS report, we see that the regional offices were very fragmented. There are around 100 regional offices, and they have not been fully evaluated. The voting mechanism in the AMS was also very complicated due to the different interests. It is important that one can respond flexibly to labour market requirements. We also have the report on the establishment of public funds and foundations on the agenda. There is also no uniform basic structure. If foundations and funds are set up, they usually create an increased administrative burden and prevent reforms because they are, of course, in doubt having an increased inventory. The Court therefore considers that criteria for the creation of funds should be laid down. In the case of reorganisation processes – in particular at the Federal Office for Water Management – we find that these processes need to be preceded by a task critique and the definition of a range of services so that organisational change can also bring about corresponding effects. On the basis of a task criticism, an analysis of personnel needs would then be carried out in order to provide administrative know-how. Third point, avoiding conflicts of interest: That is what we repeatedly see in our Court of Auditors reports. The rural development programme will promote, among other things, the operation of clusters and networks. At the project Network Kulinary, we have criticized the risk of conflicts of interest at AMA, between its owners' interests at AMA Marketing GmbH and the objective assessment of the funding case. At the same time, the Ministry's right of supervision and direction to the AMA, which as a paying agency was required to make an advance payment, existed. The Court of Auditors recommends that institutional links and incompatibilities be avoided in principle. We see such conflicts of interest, for example, in the context of quality assurance in the field of medical professional practice, because quality control is perceived by ÖQMed in Medizin GmbH, which was closely linked financially and organisationally with the medical chamber. We believe that the tasks to be carried out in the interests of the general public, namely quality assurance, are not clearly separate from the board's main task of representing the interests of doctors. Consideration could also be given to strengthening organisational independence. The fourth point concerns adequate human resources and IT support, in particular in risk areas: We have made a special audit, for example in Cum-Ex operations, on the issue of capital income tax refunds abroad, and we have complained that there is a lack of risk-adequate human resources in certain areas of financial management and that insufficient IT support and the lack of risk-oriented audits have increased the risk of multiple refunds. We believe that needs-oriented personnel could minimize the risk of tax losses. In addition, the topic of current and appropriate IT support is particularly relevant in the financial field and, for example, a modern IT application for the implementation of family support. [[Congratulations on ODP and FPÖ Members.]] Fifth point, review of effects of support measures and benefits: We have made a follow-up review in the area of transparency of benefits under income tax law and have made the central recommendation to critically search through and evaluate the benefits. We believe that, in principle, tax benefits should only be granted on a limited time-limit in order to remain effective. We repeat our long-standing recommendation that the Ministry of Finance should, within the framework of the planned tax reform, work towards a transparent, simple and understandable income tax law in order to make it easier for citizens to comply with legislation, to raise tax morality and to contribute to administrative simplification. As far as funding is concerned, we are also concerned that projects funded in the framework of cluster support should be financially sustainable. To this end, sustainable financing concepts must also be required. Finally – and that will As a National Council, you are particularly interested in legal clarifications. An audit concerns the quality of municipal budget data. We believe that the birth statistics regulation should also specify clearly what is necessary at local and national level in order to ensure that quality assurance measures can be put in place accordingly for these budgetary data. We demand the inclusion of extra-budgetary units in a comprehensive assessment of the community's conduct. In order to create legal certainty in the process of capital income tax refunds abroad, we propose that a legal regime be considered, for example, a minimum holding period of shares. This was introduced in Germany in response to the Cum-Ex business. The review of the 24-hour care has shown that the existing Article 15a agreement is no longer up-to-date and there is also a need for adaptation in this regard. The seventh issue concerns the role of public authorities. The audit report on the conservation of the Austrian forest requires that this target conflict be resolved between achieving the best possible economic success of the Federal Forest and ensuring the best possible protection of the forest by providing a corresponding ownership. In the meantime, regulations have already been set for increased care. In the context of a follow-up review, we will look at how this has really worked out. The fact is that timely conservation measures are much cheaper than technical cultivation measures if the protection effect of the forest is no longer available. Eighth, needs planning for future challenges in the care example: The Court of Auditors is currently carrying out a national audit on care. The issues of quality and financing of care are considered as a whole. [[Abg. Jarolim: Time!]] It is also a fact that in Austria we need a long-term, comprehensive planning of all care services, including the 24-hour care, in the sense of integrated and coordinated supply planning. Planning is essential across the different levels of the local authorities, based on sound data bases, forecasts and scenarios. – Thank you for the attention.

ParlaMint-AT_2019-01-30-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00060_d8e27539	Mr President. Madam President-in-Office of the Council! I would also like to refer today to the Fund and Foundations of the Federal Government and to the results presented to us by the Court of Auditors. There were around 340 foundations and funds at the time of the audits; 58 of these were reviewed, i.e. those who managed or participated in the appointment of management bodies. What is the result of this examination? – Well, it was said earlier: There was no strategy or concept that could be seen why the federal government decided to set up funds or foundations. The Court of Auditors expresses it to those who have just made it; he says that it has often been a “political decision without particular consideration of the costs and benefits of the specific legal form”. Why is this harmful now? You could say yes: My goodness, there are so many others; what is the problem? – Well, because there are rules for all things in the economy, when it makes sense to do something, and when it makes sense not to do it. For example, if there are several lenders and several decision-makers at a fund or foundation, as might be the case here – scholarships are a good example – if, for example, one has money from private donors and money from the federal government and then has to make decisions, then a foundation or a fund makes sense. But that's exactly the crux on the thing: When does it make sense and when does it make no sense? There are, of course, also disadvantages: Funds and foundations typically have less transparency to offer, and budgetary flexibility is also a little more difficult, so they are often described as inefficient. Some funds and foundations, including this, have been identified in this Court of Auditors report, were also not, although the legal requirement would be to be found in the transparency database. The Court of Auditors has made two recommendations – of course, several, but I would like to take particular note of two: The first concerns something that should be of course: Funds and foundations should only be set up if they are really appropriate and if the tasks in the existing structures cannot be performed. – It’s really sad that you have to say that in 2019, but that’s how it is now. The Court of Auditors has also recommended something second, which I very much support, namely that the existing funds and foundations should be examined as to whether they are still useful and whether they are still fulfilling everything for which they have been founded. If they don't, they should be dissolved. – We also see this very positively. We have therefore already made a request to the relevant ministries to review and analyse the foundations within their areas of competence. The answer is still pending, but we will, of course, deal with the answers accordingly. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-AT_2019-01-30-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00060_d8e27582	Mrs Rossmann is speaking on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats). - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2019-01-30-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00060_d8e27603	Mr President. High house! Madam President, I am very grateful to you for this special report by the Court of Auditors on the reimbursement of capital income taxes, because the report I have initiated has led to the discovery of tax fraud also in Austria. The series after: What's this about? – This is about so-called Cum-Ex business, which has been one of the largest tax scandals in the Federal Republic of Germany at all. Cum-Ex transactions are highly complex, highly complicated deals in which, under certain circumstances, capital income tax, once delivered to the Fiscus, is refunded twice – Cum-Ex because it is about the dividend date on which the shareholders or the owners change; as it were, these owners offer the opportunity to charge capital income tax twice, even though it has only been paid once. It was precisely this scandal in the Federal Republic of Germany that there was also a committee of inquiry that led me to make a first request to the Minister of Finance in 2015. At the time, he told me that “with high certainty” there was no harm in so-called Cum-Ex business. And this begins the chronology of a history that ends in December 2018 with the fact that the Minister of Finance in the Court of Auditors had to admit in the debate on this report that tax fraud has also occurred in Austria. In my second question in 2016, the Minister of Finance was a little more uncertain. He answered at the time: ‘ Whether or not there has been any damage in Cum/Ex cases, ongoing investigations and proceedings will show.’ I have not let go and in the years 2016 and 2017 have asked further questions: A request was sent to the Ministry of Justice, which answered me that a criminal proceedings against a particular company in connection with tax fraud is under way. The second request was made to the Ministry of Finance, and it has been found that the refund to individual foreign shareholders cannot be traced back at all. ParlaMint-AT_2019-01-30-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00060_d8e27661	Mr President. Madam President of the Court of Auditors! Ladies and gentlemen! I too would like to deal with the Court of Auditors' report on federal funds and foundations. We have already heard from Mr Doppelbauer, there are 340 funds and foundations in Austria in a close relationship with the Federal Government, countries and municipalities; 58 of them, namely those in the immediate sphere of influence of the Federal Government, have been reviewed by the Court of Auditors. In total, these 58 have a relatively high balance sheet sum, namely about €6 billion in 2014, with liabilities of around €4.4 billion. These amounts are, however, largely divided into only a few funds and foundations; I am allowed to address the most well-known ones. The first is the Environmental and Water Economic Fund. Already in 1959, with the creation of the Water Economic Fund, the promotion of environmental projects began. Since then, many, many projects in the field of water and waste water management have been carried out through the Water Economic Fund. Later, the Environment Fund came into play as a reorganisation tool in the areas of air pollution and noise protection, as well as the prevention and recovery of hazardous waste. In 1991 and 1992, a further extension of the task area to old-age rehabilitation and rehabilitation was added. A second fund is the Climate and Energy Fund. This was launched by the Federal Government in 2007 to support the implementation of the climate strategy. I would like to address a few of the points raised by the Court of Auditors: Public funds and foundations did not follow a uniform basic structure as regards the type of administration, influence of ministries, organ powers and duration, internal control and accounting rules. The Court of Auditors has also found that funds and foundations are only useful in certain constellations, especially if several lenders want to finance the performance of a task jointly and also want to have influence. It follows from this that the Court of Auditors' conclusion that half of the funds and foundations analysed are in doubt as to their effectiveness. It has been found that there is too little transparency and that budgetary flexibility, regulatory shortcomings and so on have not been taken into account accordingly. [[In the background you can hear an alarm signal.]] [[Call the SPÖ: Is the fire alarm, Mr. President?]] Ladies and gentlemen! The Court of Auditors therefore proposes that funds and foundations which no longer fulfil this purpose should be reviewed and, if necessary, dissolved and, when setting up new funds and foundations, examined in detail whether funds and foundations are, in fact, the best solution for certain tasks. Thank you very much.

It is just that we manage our forests in a timely and sustainable manner, and the Court of Auditors has examined this forest management by the Austrian Federal Forest. There have been three priorities: on the one hand, to what extent the protection effect of the forest works in the Federal Forests, on the other hand, how overall the company fulfils its obligations under the Forest Law of 1975 and the Federal Forest Act, and on the third to what extent the forests are contrary to economic efficiency. Overall, the period from 2010 to 2014 was examined, including earlier years in this report. The Court of Auditors issued a total of 19 recommendations, three specifically addressed to the Ministry, two to the Ministry and the Federal Forest and the rest to the Federal Forest alone. This report has some up-to-dateness, as I said earlier, because it is simply a matter of keeping this forest under regular management here in Austria. It is about keeping stocks stable that they are rejuvenated and released. There's a clear order: Protective forest management is a priority, and technical solutions will only be available in succession. Our state territory has 4 million hectares of forest. Of the 8.4 million hectares of land, 4 million hectares of forest are thus forest, i.e. almost half of the area in Austria is forest. Of these 4 million hectares of forest, 820,000 hectares of forest are protected, and the Federal Forests run almost 600,000 hectares of this forest and are thus the largest forestry operation in Austria. That is what I believe, from the point of view of protection, makes it quite clear from security that there is an order that we manage in time, and the Court of Auditors has already shown that, in recent years, there has been a lack of priority in the care of these forests, that too little has been invested in the protection forest and that the Federal Forests have put more power in the yield forest, especially in the management of the forest. That is why there is a clear mandate, and Mrs Bundesminister has said in the Committee that the implementation of the protection forest strategy will be a priority in the future and, fortunately, it has already begun. The focus is on natural rejuvenation of our forests, but also on the opening of areas that have not yet been opened up. In total, we can say that there is a clear mandate from this report. Some things have been implemented, some is in implementation. For the security of our country and especially for the alpine situations, I believe it is a command of the hour that we take the protection of forests and the protection of forests seriously and comply with the very clear mandate given by the Court of Auditors in the report. Now one can say that the Austrian Federal Forest makes no mistake at all, because they unfortunately have two things to pay attention to by law: One is the maintenance of the forest, so that it provides protection, the other is to harvest yields. If you have two similar tasks, it becomes difficult. That is why the Court of Auditors has clearly called for a priority of one of the two legislative tasks or has made a recommendation to that effect. I join this in, and I think it is always more important to force protection than to maximize profits. Finally, it can be said that this continuing avalanche risk in Austria once again this year has led to the purchase of helicopters worth around EUR 300 million this year under the terms of the disaster protection package. You must not misunderstand this: Of course, helicopters are important, and in the worst case you need these helicopters, of course, to save lives, but the prevention would all be a better investment, and in the ideal case, we would eventually not need so many disaster relief helicopters. Thank you.

The scope of action of each institution has been adapted to the current situation or current requirements. A transfer or transfer of the management from Vienna to Upper Austria led to a huge increase in current costs. Although the location of the various institutes was reduced, the cost increase was significant. Furthermore, according to the Court of Auditors' report, there were disagreements in the human resources sector. Thus, the academic rate was only 30 percent, although the focus of the activities was academic activity. ParlaMint-AT_2019-01-30-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00060_d8e29880	Next speaker: Mr Norbert Sieber. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2019-01-30-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00060_d8e29905	Madam President. Madam President of the Court of Auditors! Ladies and gentlemen. I am trying to be brief on the report of the Court of Auditors on the Federal Office for Water Management (Bundesamt für Wasserwirtschaft) 2018/14. The core task of the Federal Office for Water Economics is the technical support of the Federal Ministry of Sustainability and Tourism by developing foundations for legislation and implementation in the field of national and international water management. The Bundesamt für Wasserwirtschaft consists of three institutes with the following areas of competence: for the first time the Institute for Water Ecology, Fisheries Biology and Lakes, the second from the Institute for Cultural Engineering and Groundwater Budget and the third from the Institute for Aquaculture and Hydrometric Testing. The aim of the review was to assess the structure and process organisations, the performance of tasks, the performance, the economic situation and the personnel situation of the Federal Office. The period under review essentially covered the years 2011 to 2016. The Court of Auditors, following its examination, summarised nine main criticisms or recommendations. In the course of the reorganisation of the secondary departments, the areas of competence of the Federal Office for Water Management were redefined. The reorganisation was defined in the Administrative Reform Act. The aim of the reform was to create a more flatter and slimmer hierarchy for the BAW. The centrepiece was the relocation of the management of Vienna after Scharfling, associated with the merger of the management of the Federal Office and the management of the IGF. The Waldviertel eco-station was integrated into the Institute of Water Ecology, Fisheries Biology and Lakes, which enabled the cooperation in the field of aquaculture to be intensified. In principle, the Court’s suggestions have been largely implemented or the suggestions are in implementation. This, in turn, is a wonderful example of harmonious cooperation. We are very grateful to the Court of Auditors for its suggestions.
